class="small">Initial Posting: <span itemprop="datePublished">October 30, 2003</span>; Last Update: <span itemprop="dateModified">May 9, 2024</span>.</p><p><em>Estimated reading time: 58 minutes</em></p></div><div class="jig-ncbiinpagenav body-content whole_rhythm" data-jigconfig="allHeadingLevels: ['h2'],smoothScroll: false" itemprop="text"><div id="narp.Summary" itemprop="description"><h2 id="_narp_Summary_">Summary</h2><div><h4 class="inline">Clinical characteristics.</h4><p>Mitochondrial DNA-associated Leigh syndrome spectrum (mtDNA-LSS) is part of a continuum of progressive neurodegenerative disorders caused by abnormalities of mitochondrial energy generation, which includes the overlapping phenotypes mtDNA-associated Leigh syndrome and mtDNA-associated Leigh-like syndrome.</p><p>Mitochondrial DNA-LSS is characterized by onset of manifestations typically between ages three and 12 months, often following an intercurrent illness (usually viral) or metabolic challenge (vaccinations, surgery, prolonged fasting). Decompensation (often with elevated lactate levels in blood and/or cerebrospinal fluid) is typically associated with developmental delay and/or regression. Neurologic features include hypotonia, spasticity, seizures, movement disorders, cerebellar ataxia, and peripheral neuropathy. Brain stem dysfunction may manifest with respiratory symptoms, swallowing difficulties, ophthalmoparesis, and abnormalities in thermoregulation. Extraneurologic manifestations may include poor weight gain, cardiomyopathy, and conduction defects. Up to 50% of individuals die by age three years, most often from respiratory or cardiac failure.</p></div><div><h4 class="inline">Diagnosis/testing.</h4><p>The diagnosis of mtDNA-LSS is established in a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> fulfilling clinical diagnostic criteria for LSS by identification of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmic/">heteroplasmic</a> or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homoplasmic/">homoplasmic</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> in one of the 15 mtDNA genes known to be involved in mtDNA-LSS.</p></div><div><h4 class="inline">Management.</h4><p><i>Treatment of manifestations:</i> Treatment is supportive. Sodium bicarbonate or sodium citrate for significant acidosis (THAM may be used if there is hypernatraemia); anti-seizure medication for seizures; dystonia therapy with benzhexol, baclofen, tetrabenazine, or gabapentin alone or in combination, or by botulinum toxin injection; treatment of respiratory compromise per pulmonologist; caloric and nutritional supplementation and feeding therapy as needed; developmental and educational support; physical therapy and occupational therapy; standard treatment of eye movement disorders; medical management of cardiomyopathy; treatment of constipation as needed; treatment of liver failure per hepatologist; treatment of electrolyte abnormalities per nephrologist; hearing aids or cochlear implants for sensorineural hearing loss; speech therapy and hearing support services as needed; management of diabetes mellitus and adrenal insufficiency per endocrinologist; standard treatments for anxiety and/or depression; psychological support and care coordination for the affected individual and family.</p><p><i>Surveillance:</i> Affected individuals should be followed at regular intervals to monitor for progression of disease and associated complications. Neurologic assessment for ataxia and seizures at each visit along with an assessment of pulmonary issues, growth, nutrition, and gastrointestinal manifestations. Development, educational, and cognitive assessment and assessment of mobility and self-help skills at least annually. Ophthalmology assessment every six to 12 months or as advised by ophthalmologist. Annual blood pressure, EKG, and echocardiogram or as advised by cardiologist. Liver function tests, urinalysis, urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio, urine amino acids, serum electrolytes, blood urea nitrogen, creatinine, complete blood count, and fasting glucose annually. Annual audiology assessment. Assessment of care coordination and family psychosocial needs at each visit.</p><p><i>Agents/circumstances to avoid:</i> Sodium valproate, medications that cause acidosis, and dichloroacetate should be avoided or used with caution; administration of anesthesia requires careful consideration to avoid aggravation of respiratory symptoms and precipitation of respiratory failure.</p></div><div><h4 class="inline">Genetic counseling.</h4><p>Mitochondrial DNA-LSS is transmitted by maternal inheritance. The mother of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> may have the mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> and may exhibit mild clinical manifestations of mtDNA-LSS. Many affected individuals have no known family history of mtDNA-LSS or other mitochondrial disorder. The explanation for apparently <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/simplex/">simplex</a> cases may be absence of a comprehensive and/or reliable family history, a maternal <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level below the disease threshold (i.e., the minimum level of heteroplasmy expected to result in mitochondrial disease), or a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> mtDNA pathogenic variant in the proband. If the mother of the proband has the mtDNA pathogenic variant identified in the proband, all sibs of the proband are at risk of inheriting the pathogenic variant. Sibs may inherit the pathogenic variant at varying heteroplasmy levels due to the bottleneck effect and variant-specific <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/segregation/">segregation</a> patterns. The risk to a sib of developing clinical manifestations is difficult to determine and depends on heteroplasmy level, the variation in heteroplasmy levels among different tissues, and the disease threshold for the specific variant. Recurrence risk assessment and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/prenatal-testing/">prenatal testing</a> for disorders caused by pathogenic variants in mtDNA is challenging due to the intricacies of mtDNA transmission and the inherent challenge in using prenatal genetic test results to predict clinical outcome. Reproductive options for the family members of a proband with an mtDNA pathogenic variant may include prenatal testing, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a>, and oocyte donation.</p></div></div><div id="narp.Diagnosis"><h2 id="_narp_Diagnosis_">Diagnosis</h2><p>Diagnostic criteria for Leigh syndrome spectrum (LSS) have been published [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.mccormick.2023.696">McCormick et al 2023</a>].</p><div id="narp.Suggestive_Findings"><h3>Suggestive Findings</h3><p><b>Mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA)-associated LSS (mtDNA-LSS) should be suspected</b> in probands with several of the following clinical, laboratory, and/or imaging findings.</p><p>
<b>Clinical findings</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Progressive neurologic disease with developmental delay and neurodevelopmental regression</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Manifestations of brain stem and/or basal ganglia disease (e.g., respiratory abnormalities, nystagmus, ophthalmoparesis, optic atrophy, ataxia, and dystonia)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Seizures</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Psychiatric disturbance</div></li></ul><p>
<b>Laboratory findings</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Elevated blood lactate</div><div class="half_rhythm">Elevation tends to be more marked in postprandial samples.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Elevated cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) lactate</div><div class="half_rhythm">Note: While elevated blood and/or CSF lactate is suggestive of a mitochondrial disorder, a normal lactate does not exclude mtDNA-LSS.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Plasma amino acids may show increased alanine concentration, reflecting persistent hyperlactatemia. Decreased plasma citrulline concentration and/or elevated 3-hydroxy-isovalerylcarnitine (C5-OH) levels has been reported in individuals with <i>MT-ATP6</i> pathogenic variants [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.balasubramaniam.2017.99">Balasubramaniam et al 2017</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.larson.2019.58">Larson et al 2019</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.peretz.2021.37">Peretz et al 2021</a>,<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.tise.2023.1492">Tise et al 2023</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Urine organic acid analysis results are often nonspecific and may show lactic aciduria, increased Krebs cycle intermediates, and increased dicarboxylic acids.</div><div class="half_rhythm">Note: 3-methylglutaconic aciduria may be observed in some nuclear <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-encoded causes of LSS, notably 3-<b><i>me</i></b>thyl<b><i>g</i></b>lutaconic aciduria with <b><i>d</i></b>eafness-dystonia, [hepatopathy], <b><i>e</i></b>ncephalomyopathy, and <b><i>L</i></b>eigh-like syndrome (MEGD[H]EL syndrome caused by <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/megdel/">SERAC1 deficiency</a>), and ethylmalonic aciduria is a feature of ethylmalonic encephalopathy caused by <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/biallelic/">biallelic</a> variants in <i>ETHE1</i>. Identification of increased methylmalonic acid or propionic acid is suggestive of other genetic causes of LSS or organic acidemias (e.g., <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/suclg1-mtdepl/"><i>SUCLG1</i>-</a> or <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/sucla2-def/"><i>SUCLA2</i>-related mtDNA depletion syndrome</a>, <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/mma/">methylmalonic acidemia</a>, <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/propionic-a/">propionic acidemia</a>) (see <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/leigh-nucl-ov/#leigh-nucl-ov.Causes_of_Nuclear_GeneEnco">Causes of Nuclear Gene-Encoded Leigh Syndrome Spectrum</a> and <a href="#narp.Differential_Diagnosis">Differential Diagnosis</a>).</div></li></ul><p>
<b>Brain imaging findings</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Characteristic findings include bilateral symmetric hyperintense signal abnormality in the brain stem and/or basal ganglia on T<sub>2</sub>-weighted MRI or bilateral symmetric hypodensities in the basal ganglia on CT that may progress/fluctuate with time [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.bonfante.2016.443">Bonfante et al 2016</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.alves.2020.218">Alves et al 2020</a>]. Corresponding restricted diffusion may be evident on diffusion-weighted imaging [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.alves.2020.218">Alves et al 2020</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Additional findings can include cerebral atrophy, bilateral hyperintense signal abnormality in the thalamus, cerebellum, cerebral white matter, and/or spinal cord on T<sub>2</sub>-weighted MRI [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.bonfante.2016.443">Bonfante et al 2016</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.alves.2020.218">Alves et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.hong.2020.267">Hong et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.stenton.2022.466">Stenton et al 2022</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="half_rhythm">Magnetic resonance spectroscopy (MRS) lactate peak (in the absence of acute seizures)</div><div class="half_rhythm">Note: Characteristic patterns of brain lesions have been described for specific causes of nuclear <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-encoded LSS. For example, specific brain lesions affecting the mammillothalamic tracts, substantia nigra, medial lemniscus, medial longitudinal fasciculus, spinothalamic tracts, and cerebellum appear to be characteristic of <i>NDUFAF2</i>-related LSS. <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/megdel/">SERAC1 deficiency</a> is associated with a distinctive brain MRI pattern affecting the basal ganglia, especially the putamen. Initially there are T<sub>2</sub>-weighted signal changes of the pallidum, and later swelling of the putamen and caudate nucleus with an "eye" representing early sparing of the dorsal putamen, followed by progressive involvement of the putamina (see <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/leigh-nucl-ov/#leigh-nucl-ov.Causes_of_Nuclear_GeneEnco">Causes of Nuclear Gene-Encoded Leigh Syndrome Spectrum</a>).</div></li></ul><p><b>Histopathology</b>
<b>of muscle tissue</b> may be normal or may show nonspecific changes such as accumulation of intracytoplasmic neutral lipid droplets or predominance of type II muscle fibers. Ragged red fibers and/or cytochrome <i>c</i> oxidase-negative fibers may occasionally be observed.</p><p>Since the advent of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/next-generation-sequencing/">next-generation sequencing</a>, muscle biopsy is performed less frequently (especially in the pediatric population), as obtaining a muscle biopsy is invasive and often requires general anesthesia, which carries a risk of metabolic decompensation. Muscle biopsy is now generally reserved for critically unwell individuals or (occasionally) for functional validation of genetic findings.</p><p>Note: (1) Although muscle histopathology is only occasionally abnormal, when it is abnormal it can be as much of a contributor to diagnostic certainty as respiratory chain enzymes or molecular testing. (2) If muscle biopsy is obtained for respiratory chain enzyme studies, histopathology should also be performed.</p><p><b>Respiratory chain enzyme studies.</b> Biochemical analysis of tissue biopsies or cultured cells often detects deficient activity (&#x0003c;30% of control mean values) of one or more of the respiratory chain enzyme complexes. Isolated defects of complex I or complex IV are the most common enzyme abnormalities observed in individuals with LSS and can help guide subsequent <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/molecular-genetic-testing/">molecular genetic testing</a> of mtDNA or nuclear genes [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.sofou.2018.21">Sofou et al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.hong.2020.267">Hong et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.ogawa.2020.819">Ogawa et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.ardissone.2021.413">Ardissone et al 2021</a>]. Combined respiratory chain enzyme defects can also be observed, particularly in disorders of mitochondrial <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> expression. Normal respiratory chain enzyme analysis does not exclude mtDNA-LSS and can be seen in individuals with mtDNA pathogenic variants affecting complex V subunits such as the <i>MT-ATP6</i> pathogenic variants <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.8993T&#x0003e;G</a>, <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.8993T&#x0003e;C</a>, and <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.9176T&#x0003e;C</a>.</p><p>Note: (1) Skeletal muscle (vastus lateralis) is usually the tissue of choice for respiratory chain enzyme studies. (2) Skin fibroblasts can be used, but only about 50% of respiratory chain enzyme defects identified in skeletal muscle are also identified in skin fibroblasts. (3) Approximately 10%-20% of individuals with normal skeletal muscle respiratory chain enzyme analysis may have an enzyme defect detected in liver or cardiac muscle, particularly if those tissues are involved clinically [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.frazier.2020.121">Frazier et al 2020</a>].</p></div><div id="narp.Establishing_the_Diagnosis"><h3>Establishing the Diagnosis</h3><p>The diagnosis of mtDNA-LSS <b>is established</b> in a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> fulfilling criteria for LSS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.mccormick.2023.696">McCormick et al 2023</a>] in whom a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmic/">heteroplasmic</a> or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homoplasmic/">homoplasmic</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> in one of the genes listed in <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTmoleculargeneticsofmitochondri">Table 1</a> has been identified.</p><div id="narp.Clinical_Criteria_for_mtDNALSS"><h4>Clinical Criteria for mtDNA-LSS</h4><p><b>Typical neuroradiologic findings</b>. Bilateral, typically symmetric hyperintensities on T<sub>2</sub>-weighted MRI or hypodensities on CT in the brain stem and/or basal ganglia with or without bilateral, T<sub>2</sub>-weighted hyperintensities on MRI or hypodensities on CT in the thalamus, cerebellum, subcortical white matter, and/or spinal cord</p><p><b>AND</b> &#x02265;1 of the following <b>characteristic neurologic clinical features</b>:</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Developmental regression</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Developmental delay</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Psychiatric features</div></li></ul><p><b>AND</b> &#x02265;1 of the following <b>biochemical and/or mitochondrial abnormalities</b>:</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Elevated lactate in plasma and/or CSF</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>MRS lactate peak (in absence of acute seizures)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Respiratory chain enzyme activity deficiency (&#x0003c;30% enzyme activity) in affected tissues (muscle, liver, fibroblasts)</div></li></ul><p>Note: Historically, the diagnosis of LSS was achieved by demonstrating mitochondrial dysfunction through relevant tissue biopsy to inform a targeted molecular genetic approach. However, the introduction of comprehensive <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genomic/">genomic</a> sequencing has removed the need for tissue biopsy in most scenarios.</p></div><div id="narp.Molecular_Genetic_Testing"><h4>Molecular Genetic Testing</h4><p>Molecular genetic testing approaches for mtDNA-LSS can include <b>comprehensive</b>
<b><a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genomic/">genomic</a> testing</b> (<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genome-sequencing/">genome sequencing</a> or mtDNA sequencing and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exome-sequencing/">exome sequencing</a>) or <b>targeted single-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> testing</b>. Choice of approach may be dependent on clinical suspicion, age of presentation, and availability of genomic testing.</p><div id="narp.Option_1"><h5>
<b>Option 1</b>
</h5><p>Comprehensive <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genomic/">genomic</a> testing includes sequencing of mtDNA and nuclear DNA. Comprehensive genomic testing does not require a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a>-driven hypothesis and may provide or suggest a diagnosis not previously considered (e.g., variant in a different <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>[s] that results in a similar clinical presentation).</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Genome sequencing is preferred (if available), as it allows analysis of the mitochondrial and nuclear genome, and typically has the highest diagnostic rate.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>If <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genome-sequencing/">genome sequencing</a> is not available, mtDNA sequencing is recommended. Exome sequencing should be considered in conjunction with mtDNA sequencing, especially in the pediatric population, given that approximately 70% of individuals will have nuclear <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-encoded LSS (see <a href="#narp.Differential_Diagnosis">Differential Diagnosis</a>). For children, a parent-child trio should be considered to assist in the identification of rare <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-recessive/">autosomal recessive</a> or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> pathogenic variants. It may be possible to extract mtDNA sequence data from some <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exome/">exome</a> kits, but coverage varies and may not be of clinical quality, hence stand-alone mtDNA sequencing is recommended.</div></li></ul><p>For an introduction to comprehensive <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genomic/">genomic</a> testing click <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app5/#app5.Comprehensive_Genomic_Testing">here</a>. More detailed information for clinicians ordering genomic testing can be found <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app5/#app5.Comprehensive_Genomic_Testing_1">here</a>.</p></div><div id="narp.Option_2"><h5>
<b>Option 2</b>
</h5><p>Targeted analysis of common pathogenic variants may be considered if there is a high clinical suspicion based on clinical or biochemical findings or if comprehensive testing is not available (see <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTmoleculargeneticsofmitochondri">Table 1</a>).</p><p>Note: (1) Most mtDNA pathogenic variants associated with mtDNA-LSS are "<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmic/">heteroplasmic</a>" (i.e., abnormal mtDNA coexists with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/wild_type/">wild type</a> mtDNA) and for some pathogenic variants, the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level may vary among different tissues and may increase or decrease with age. (2) Mitochondrial DNA pathogenic variants may be lost from the leukocyte population with increasing age [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.schon.2021.e066288">Schon et al 2021</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.davis.2022.e730">Davis et al 2022</a>]; therefore, in adults with milder manifestations and asymptomatic older maternal relatives, the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> may only be detected in hair follicles, urine sediment cells, or skeletal muscle, which is the most reliable source of mtDNA [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.davis.2022.e730">Davis et al 2022</a>]. (3) Leukocyte testing is recommended in children, particularly when using <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/next-generation-sequencing/">next-generation sequencing</a> methods, which allow detection of very low heteroplasmy levels. (4) Long-range <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pcr/">PCR</a> can be performed to identify large mtDNA rearrangements. (5) Mitochondrial DNA deletions are not usually detectable in leukocyte DNA in adults; in this age group, skeletal muscle (or urinary sediment cells) is the tissue of choice for analysis [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.schon.2021.e066288">Schon et al 2021</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.davis.2022.e730">Davis et al 2022</a>]. (6) Deletions/duplications of mtDNA are an extremely rare cause of LSS in adults. (7) The authors are not aware of any published reports of children with mtDNA-LSS when the pathogenic variant was present in another tissue but not detected in blood.</p><div headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-ATP6</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~40%&#x000a0;<sup>8</sup></td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_3 hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_2_1" rowspan="15" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x0003e;95%</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_3 hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_2_2" rowspan="15" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x0003c;5%&#x000a0;<sup>9</sup></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-ND3</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~20%</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-ND5</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~15%</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-ND6</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~10%</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-ND4</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">~5%</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-ND1</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x0003c;5%</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-TL1</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">&#x0003c;5%</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-CO1</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Rare</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-CO2</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Rare</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-CO3</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Rare</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-ND2</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Rare</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-TI</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Rare</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-TK</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Rare</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-TV</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Rare</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<i>MT-TW</i>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.molecular_genetics_of_mitochondri_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Rare</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">mtDNA-LSS = mitochondrial DNA-associated Leigh syndrome spectrum</p></div></dd><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.1.1"><p class="no_margin">Genes are listed from most frequent to least frequent genetic cause of mtDNA-LSS and then alphabetically.</p></div></dd><dt>2. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.1.2"><p class="no_margin">See <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/#narp.molgen.TA">Table A. Genes and Databases</a> for <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/chromosome/">chromosome</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/locus/">locus</a> and protein.</p></div></dd><dt>3. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.1.3"><p class="no_margin">These estimates are based on several cohorts of individuals with mtDNA-LSS, which are likely affected by inclusion criteria, testing strategies, and ascertainment bias [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.sofou.2014.52">Sofou et al 2014</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.bonfante.2016.443">Bonfante et al 2016</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.sofou.2018.21">Sofou et al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.wei.2018.2388">Wei et al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.yu.2018.2705">Yu et al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.alves.2020.218">Alves et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.hong.2020.267">Hong et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.lee.2020.586">Lee et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.ogawa.2020.819">Ogawa et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.ardissone.2021.413">Ardissone et al 2021</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.lim.2022.117">Lim et al 2022</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.stenton.2022.466">Stenton et al 2022</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.tinker.2022.342">Tinker et al 2022</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.ardissone.2023.2079">Ardissone et al 2023</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.baldo.2023.27">Baldo et al 2023</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.kistol.2023.1597">Kistol et al 2023</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.na.2023.27">Na &#x00026; Lee 2023</a>].</p></div></dd><dt>4. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.1.4"><p class="no_margin">Data derived from <a href="https://mitomap.org/MITOMAP" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">MITOMAP</a></p></div></dd><dt>5. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.1.5"><p class="no_margin">See <a href="#narp.Molecular_Genetics">Molecular Genetics</a> for information on pathogenic variants detected.</p></div></dd><dt>6. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.1.6"><p class="no_margin">Sequence analysis detects variants that are benign, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-benign/">likely benign</a>, of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/uncertain-significance/">uncertain significance</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/likely-pathogenic/">likely pathogenic</a>, or pathogenic. Variants may include <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/missense/">missense</a> and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/nonsense-variant/">nonsense</a> variants and small deletions/insertions; typically, larger deletions/duplications are not detected. For issues to consider in interpretation of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/sequence-analysis/">sequence analysis</a> results, click <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app2/">here</a>.</p></div></dd><dt>7. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.1.7"><p class="no_margin">Single-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a> mtDNA deletions have not been reported. Deletion/<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/duplication/">duplication</a> analysis of the entire mitochondrial genome may include a range of techniques such as <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/quantitative-pcr/">quantitative PCR</a> or long-range PCR.</p></div></dd><dt>8. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.1.8"><p class="no_margin">The most common <i>MT-ATP6</i> pathogenic variants in individuals with mtDNA-LSS include <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.8993T&#x0003e;G</a>, <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.8993T&#x0003e;C</a>, <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.9176T&#x0003e;C</a>, and <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.9185T&#x0003e;C</a> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.ganetzky.2019.499">Ganetzky et al 2019</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.stendel.2020.e393">Stendel et al 2020</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.na.2022.414">Na &#x00026; Lee 2022</a>].</p></div></dd><dt>9. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.1.9"><p class="no_margin">Large-scale deletions have been reported in individuals with mtDNA-LSS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.yamashita.2008.598">Yamashita et al 2008</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.ardissone.2021.413">Ardissone et al 2021</a>].</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div></div></div></div><div id="narp.Clinical_Characteristics"><h2 id="_narp_Clinical_Characteristics_">Clinical Characteristics</h2><div id="narp.Clinical_Description"><h3>Clinical Description</h3><p>Mitochondrial DNA-associated Leigh syndrome spectrum (mtDNA-LSS) is a continuum of progressive neurodegenerative disorders, with typical onset in infancy or early childhood of sudden neurodevelopmental regression. Individuals with LSS may have variable extraneurologic manifestations including gastrointestinal, cardiac, hepatic, renal, and endocrine disease. The clinical features of mtDNA-LSS are similar to those observed in individuals with nuclear <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-encoded Leigh syndrome spectrum (LSS). Clinically, it is difficult to distinguish mtDNA-LSS from nuclear gene-encoded LSS, and there are relatively few publications describing the full range of clinical features in mtDNA-LSS. The following description of the phenotypic features summarizes the more robust data on features of all individuals with LSS; specific information and references for mtDNA-LSS are included if available.</p><div id="narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 2. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Mitochondrial DNA-Associated Leigh Syndrome Spectrum: Frequency of Select Features</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" <b>Neurologic features</b>
