<ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Community or <a href="#narp.Resources">online resources</a> such as <a href="https://www.p2pusa.org/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Parent to Parent</a></div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Social work involvement for parental support</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Home nursing referral</div></li></ul>
</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">ADL = activities of daily living; BUN = blood urea nitrogen; CSF = cerebrospinal fluid; MOI = <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/mode-of-inheritance/">mode of inheritance</a>; MRS = magnetic resonance spectroscopy; mtDNA-LSS = mitochondrial DNA-associated Leigh syndrome spectrum; OT = occupational therapy; PT = physical therapy</p></div></dd><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.5.1"><p class="no_margin">Medical geneticist, certified genetic counselor, certified advanced genetic nurse</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div><div id="narp.Treatment_of_Manifestations"><h3>Treatment of Manifestations</h3><p>As for most mitochondrial disorders, no specific curative treatment for mtDNA-LSS exists [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.pitceathly.2021.22">Pitceathly et al 2021</a>], and currently there are no licensed or approved treatments for mtDNA-LSS. Supportive care to improve quality of life, maximize function, and reduce complications is recommended. This ideally involves multidisciplinary care by specialists in relevant fields (see <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTmitochondrialdnaassociatedleigh2">Table 6</a>).</p><div id="narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 6. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Mitochondrial DNA-Associated Leigh Syndrome Spectrum: Treatment of Manifestations</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Manifestation/Concern</th><th id="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Treatment</th><th id="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Considerations/Other</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Acute acidosis</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Sodium bicarbonate or sodium citrate for significant acidosis</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Consider THAM if accompanying hypernatremia.</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Avoid lactate containing solutions (e.g., Ringer lactate).</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Epilepsy</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Treatment w/ASM by experienced neurologist</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Sodium valproate should be avoided because of its inhibitory effects on mitochondrial respiratory chain.&#x000a0;<sup>1</sup></div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Education of parents/caregivers&#x000a0;<sup>2</sup></div></li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" rowspan="2" scope="row" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Dystonia</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Benzhexol, baclofen, tetrabenazine, &#x00026; gabapentin may be useful, alone or in various combinations.</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">An initial low dose should be started &#x00026; gradually &#x02191; until symptom control is achieved, or intolerable side effects occur.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" colspan="1" scope="row" rowspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Botulinum toxin injection has been used in persons w/LSS &#x00026; severe intractable dystonia.</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Use w/caution given potential side effects of exacerbating muscle weakness; &#x00026; do not use in head &#x00026; neck because of risk of adverse events (e.g., impaired swallow).</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Pulmonology</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Referral to pulmonologist as needed</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Ventilatory support for persons w/respiratory compromise</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Feeding/Nutrition</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Caloric &#x00026; nutritional supplementation as needed (incl micronutrients)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Feeding therapy as needed</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Gastrostomy tube placement may improve nutritional intake for those w/persistent feeding issues, choking, or aspiration risk due to dysphagia.</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Low threshold for clinical feeding eval &#x00026;/or radiographic swallowing study when showing clinical signs or symptoms of dysphagia</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Development/Cognition</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">See <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/bin/narp-dd_id_mgmt.pdf">Developmental Delay&#x000a0;/ Intellectual Disability Management Issues</a>.</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Myopathy &#x00026;/or ataxia</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">PT &#x00026; OT</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Equipment to prevent falling &#x00026; maintain independent mobility as appropriate</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Eyes</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Standard treatments for eye movement disorders as advised by ophthalmologist</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Cardiomyopathy</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Anticongestive therapy or antiarrhythmic therapy may be required &#x00026; treatment should be managed by cardiologist.</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Gastrointestinal</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Stool softeners, laxatives for constipation as needed</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Liver</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Treatments for liver failure as advised by hepatologist</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Renal</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Electrolyte monitoring &#x00026; replacement for renal tubular losses per nephrologist</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Hearing</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Hearing aids or cochlear implants for sensorineural hearing loss</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Referral to speech therapy</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Referral to hearing support services</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Counsel to avoid excessive noise exposure, which can exacerbate hearing loss.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Endocrine</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Treatment for diabetes mellitus or adrenal insufficiency managed by endocrinologist</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Psychosocial</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Standard treatments of anxiety &#x00026;/or depression</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"></td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Family/Community</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Ensure appropriate social work involvement to connect families w/local resources, respite, &#x00026; support.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Coordinate care to manage multiple subspecialty appointments, equipment, medications, &#x00026; supplies.</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_2_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Ongoing assessment of need for palliative care involvement &#x00026;/or home nursing</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">ASM = anti-seizure medication; OT = occupational therapy; PT = physical therapy; THAM = tris-hydroxymethyl aminomethane</p></div></dd><dt>1. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.6.1"><p class="no_margin">
<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.de_vries.2020.800">De Vries et al [2020]</a>
</p></div></dd><dt>2. </dt><dd><div id="narp.TF.6.2"><p class="no_margin">Education of parents/caregivers regarding common seizure presentations is appropriate. For information on non-medical interventions and coping strategies for children diagnosed with epilepsy, see <a href="https://www.epilepsy.com/tools-resources/forms-resources#Epilepsy-Foundation-Toolbox" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Epilepsy Foundation Toolbox</a>.</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div><div id="narp.Surveillance"><h3>Surveillance</h3><p>To monitor existing manifestations, the individual's response to supportive care, and the emergence of new manifestations, the evaluations summarized in <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTmitochondrialdnaassociatedleigh3">Table 7</a> are recommended.</p><div id="narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3" class="table"><h3><span class="label">Table 7. </span></h3><div class="caption"><p>Mitochondrial DNA-Associated Leigh Syndrome Spectrum: Recommended Surveillance</p></div><p class="large-table-link" style="display:none"><span class="right"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3/?report=objectonly" target="object">View in own window</a></span></p><div class="large_tbl" id="__narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_lrgtbl__"><table class="no_bottom_margin"><thead><tr><th id="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">System/Concern</th><th id="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Evaluation</th><th id="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_3" scope="col" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Frequency</th></tr></thead><tbody><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Neurologic</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Neurology assessment for ataxia/movement disorders, neuropathy, &#x00026; seizures</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_3" rowspan="4" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">At each visit</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Pulmonary</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Monitor for evidence of aspiration, nocturnal hypoventilation, or sleep-disordered breathing.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Feeding/Nutrition</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Measurement of growth parameters</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Eval of nutritional status (incl micronutrients) &#x00026; safety of oral intake</div></li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Gastroenterology</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Assess for nausea, constipation, or dysmotility.</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Development/</b>
<br />
<b>Cognition</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Monitor developmental progress, educational needs, &#x00026; cognitive issues.</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_3" rowspan="2" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">At least annually</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Musculoskeletal</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Physical medicine, OT/PT assessment of mobility, self-help skills</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Ophthalmology</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Ophthalmologic eval</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Every 6-12 mos or as advised by ophthalmologist</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Cardiac</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Blood pressure</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>EKG</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Echocardiogram</div></li></ul>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Annually or as advised by cardiologist</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Liver</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Liver function tests incl transaminases, albumin, bilirubin, &#x00026; coagulation studies</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_3" rowspan="5" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Annually</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Renal</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Urinalysis, urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio, urine amino acids</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Serum electrolytes, BUN, creatinine</div></li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Hearing</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Audiologic eval</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Hematology</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">Complete blood count</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Endocrine</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;"><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Fasting glucose</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Consider hemoglobin A1c or OGTT if clinical features consistent w/diabetes mellitus.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Consider early-morning cortisol &#x00026; short synacthen test if clinical symptoms of adrenal insufficiency.</div></li></ul>
</td></tr><tr><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_1" scope="row" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">
<b>Family/Community</b>
</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_2" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">Assess family need for social work support (e.g., palliative/respite care, home nursing, other local resources), care coordination, or follow-up <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genetic-counseling/">genetic counseling</a> if new questions arise (e.g., family planning).</td><td headers="hd_h_narp.T.mitochondrial_dnaassociated_leigh_3_1_1_1_3" rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:middle;">At each visit</td></tr></tbody></table></div><div><div><dl class="temp-labeled-list small"><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">BUN = blood urea nitrogen; OGTT = oral glucose tolerance test; OT = occupational therapy; PT = physical therapy</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div><div id="narp.AgentsCircumstances_to_Avoid"><h3>Agents/Circumstances to Avoid</h3><p>An international Delphi-based consensus on the safety of medication use in individuals with primary mitochondrial disorders was published in 2020 to assist clinical decision making. However, it is important to tailor medication recommendations to the individual [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.de_vries.2020.800">De Vries et al 2020</a>].</p><p><b>Sodium valproate</b> should be avoided if possible, because of its inhibitory effect on respiratory chain enzymes.</p><p><b>Medications that can cause acidosis</b> should be avoided or, if necessary, prescribed with caution, with frequent monitoring of blood acid-base levels.</p><p><b>Anesthesia</b> can potentially aggravate respiratory symptoms and precipitate respiratory failure, so careful consideration should be given to its use and to monitoring of the individual prior to, during, and after anesthetic procedures [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.parikh.2017.1380">Parikh et al 2017</a>]. Catabolism should be prevented by minimizing preoperative fasting and considering intravenous glucose perioperatively during prolonged anesthesia (unless the individual is on a ketogenic diet). Prolonged propofol use during maintenance anesthesia may increase the risk of lactic acidosis. Neuromuscular blocking drugs should be avoided in individuals with muscle disease, or if necessary, under strict monitoring. Avoid lactate-containing agents (e.g., Ringer lactate) in those with risk of lactic acidosis [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.parikh.2017.1380">Parikh et al 2017</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.de_vries.2020.800">De Vries et al 2020</a>].</p><p><b>Dichloroacetate (DCA)</b> reduces blood lactate by activating the pyruvate dehydrogenase complex. Mixed clinical outcomes have been observed following the use of DCA in individuals with mitochondrial disorders. DCA was reported to worsen peripheral neuropathy in a randomized clinical trial in individuals with MELAS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.kaufmann.2006.324">Kaufmann et al 2006</a>]. It is therefore recommended that DCA should be used with caution in individuals with LSS, particularly in individuals with established peripheral neuropathy or with a genetic cause associated with increased risk of peripheral neuropathy.</p></div><div id="narp.Evaluation_of_Relatives_at_Risk"><h3>Evaluation of Relatives at Risk</h3><p>See <a href="#narp.Related_Genetic_Counseling_Issues">Genetic Counseling</a> for issues related to testing of at-risk relatives for <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genetic-counseling/">genetic counseling</a> purposes.</p></div><div id="narp.Therapies_Under_Investigation"><h3>Therapies Under Investigation</h3><p>A number of vitamins, cofactors, and other compounds, including riboflavin, thiamine, creatine, biotin, coenzyme Q<sub>10</sub>, carnitine, and alpha-lipoic acid, have been prescribed in individuals with mtDNA-LSS. To date, there is no consistency in prescribing practice between international centers. Despite the theoretic potential, there is no established evidence base for any of these compounds in mtDNA-LSS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.pfeffer.2012b.cd004426">Pfeffer et al 2012b</a>]. Complex phenotypic and genetic heterogeneity, as well as a lack of robust clinical outcome measures, are some of the obstacles to developing effective therapies for mtDNA-LSS. Evidence for some of these therapies has been shown in specific vitamin/cofactor metabolism and transporter defects in <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/leigh-nucl-ov/">nuclear gene-encoded Leigh syndrome spectrum</a>. In recent years, there has been an increase in clinical trials for pharmacologic and non-pharmacologic therapies for mtDNA-LSS.</p><p><b>Antioxidants</b> such as coenzyme Q<sub>10</sub> and its analogs have demonstrated potential antioxidant activities against toxic metabolites and accumulated reactive oxygen species. Apart from disorders of coenzyme Q<sub>10</sub> biosynthesis, coenzyme Q<sub>10</sub> has limited evidence for efficacy in the treatment of LSS.</p><p>Vatiquinone (PTC-743; previously known as EPI-743) is a structurally modified variant of coenzyme Q<sub>10</sub> that may help to replenish intracellular glutathione levels [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.shrader.2011.3693">Shrader et al 2011</a>]. An open-label clinical trial in an end-of-life setting in 14 individuals with primary mitochondrial disorders (including mtDNA-LSS) showed clinical improvement in 11 of the 14 individuals, which corresponded to increased regional and whole-brain technetium-99m hexamethylpropylene oxime uptake on single-photon emission computed tomography [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.enns.2012.91">Enns et al 2012</a>]. An open-label Phase IIA single-center clinical trial of vatiquinone therapy showed stabilization of disease progression in ten children with mtDNA-LSS or nuclear <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-encoded LSS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.martinelli.2012.383">Martinelli et al 2012</a>]. The unpredictable natural history of mtDNA-LSS causes difficulty in interpretation of open-label studies. A Phase IIB randomized placebo-controlled double-blind crossover clinical trial of vatiquinone in children with mtDNA-LSS or nuclear gene-encoded LSS was completed in May 2015 (<a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT01721733" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">NCT01721733</a>), and a long-term safety and efficacy evaluation of vatiquinone in children with mtDNA-LSS or nuclear gene-encoded LSS was completed in late 2023 (<a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT02352896" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">NCT02352896</a>). The findings of these studies have not yet been published. A recent clinical trial of vatiquinone in individuals with mitochondrial disorders with associated epilepsy <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> (<a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT04378075" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">NCT04378075</a>) failed to meet its primary end point (see PTC Therapeutics <a href="https://www.prnewswire.com/news-releases/ptc-therapeutics-announces-results-from-mit-e-clinical-trial-of-vatiquinone-for-the-treatment-of-mitochondrial-disease-associated-seizures-301867528.html#:~:text=SOUTH%20PLAINFIELD%2C%20N.J.%2C%20June%2029,reduction%20in%20observable%20motor%20seizures" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">press release</a>).</p><p>KH176 (sonlicromanol) is a redox-modulating agent that targets the thioredoxin/peroxiredoxin system to reduce cellular reactive oxygen species. Promising preclinical studies in a mouse model for LSS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.de_haas.2017.11733">de Haas et al 2017</a>] led to a Phase I study, which reported that KH176 was tolerated but at higher doses was associated with changes in cardiac electrophysiology (QTc prolongation and T wave morphologic changes) [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.koene.2017.163">Koene et al 2017</a>]. A Phase II randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group clinical trial in children with genetically confirmed primary mitochondrial disorders with motor symptoms is currently recruiting (<a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT04846036" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">NCT04846036</a>).</p><p><b>Gene therapy.</b> Gene-editing techniques with mitochondrial targeted restriction endonucleases (mitoREs) or programmable nucleases, such as mitochondrial targeted zinc finger nucleases (mtZFN) and transcription activator-like effector nucleases (mito-TALEN), are postulated to shift <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> toward <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/wild_type/">wild type</a> mtDNA by inducing mtDNA double-stranded breaks, leading to rapid degradation of mutated mtDNA. Gene-editing techniques that introduce specific <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> nucleotide changes in mtDNA include bacterial cytidine deaminases. However, to date, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene-therapy/">gene therapy</a> has not progressed to clinical trials in humans.</p><p>Search <a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">ClinicalTrials.gov</a> in the US and <a href="https://www.clinicaltrialsregister.eu/ctr-search/search" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">EU Clinical Trials Register</a> in Europe for access to information on clinical studies for a wide range of diseases and conditions.</p></div><div id="narp.Other"><h3>Other</h3><p><b>Ketogenic diet</b> (KD) has been proposed as a therapy for mitochondrial disorders, in particular complex I deficiency [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.paleologou.2017.56">Paleologou et al 2017</a>]. Although this high-fat, low-carbohydrate diet has proven efficacy for refractory epilepsies, high-quality evidence for its use in the treatment of primary mitochondrial disorders, including mtDNA-LSS, is currently lacking. Studies in patient-derived fibroblasts have postulated that KD may be beneficial in mitochondrial disorders [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.santra.2004.662">Santra et al 2004</a>]. A systematic review on the use of KD in mitochondrial disorders highlighted the paucity of high-quality studies but suggested that KD can be considered in individuals with mitochondrial disorders (including mtDNA-LSS) and therapy-refractory epilepsy (except mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion/">deletion</a>-related myopathy and pyruvate carboxylase deficiency) [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.zweers.2021.295">Zweers et al 2021</a>]. A prospective controlled study in individuals with mitochondrial disorders (including one individual with LSS) found that KD reduced seizure frequency in 76%, with a high efficacy in individuals with MELAS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.huang.2022.880944">Huang et al 2022</a>]. Further clinical trials are required to determine the efficacy of KD in mtDNA-LSS. Another proposed option is supplementation with the fatty acid decanoic acid, which is thought to be the active component of the KD and appears to stimulate mitochondrial biogenesis in cell models [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.hughes.2014.426">Hughes et al 2014</a>], including fibroblasts from individuals with complex I-deficient nuclear <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-encoded LSS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.kanabus.2016.415">Kanabus et al 2016</a>]. A prospective open-label feasibility study of K.Vita, a medical food containing a unique ratio of decanoic acid to octanoic acid, had a beneficial effect on frequency of seizures, including in individuals with primary mitochondrial disorders [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.schoeler.2021">Schoeler et al 2021</a>]. Further clinical trials are needed.</p><p><b>Mitochondrial biogenesis</b> is the process in which cells increase the mitochondrial mass. Several studies have investigated whether upregulation of mitochondrial biogenesis may provide an effective therapeutic approach for mitochondrial respiratory chain disorders. This approach involves using agonists such as bezafibrate or resveratrol to stimulate the peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor gamma (PPARgamma) coactivator alpha (PGC-1a) pathway [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.bastin.2008.1433">Bastin et al 2008</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.steele.2020.e11589">Steele et al 2020</a>]. Alternatively, nicotinamide analogs such as nicotinamide riboside or nicotinamide mononucleotide have been used to boost reduced nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide (NAD<sup>+</sup>) levels and induce mitochondrial biogenesis via the same PGC-1a pathway [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.cerutti.2014.1042">Cerutti et al 2014</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.khan.2014.721">Khan et al 2014</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.felici.2015.965">Felici et al 2015</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.long.2015.19">Long et al 2015</a>]. KL1333, a novel NAD<sup>+</sup> modulator, interacts with NAD(P)H:dehydrogenase 1 (NQO1) to increase intracellular NAD<sup>+</sup> levels via NADH oxidation [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.seo.2018.552">Seo et al 2018</a>]. Results of a Phase Ia/Ib trial assessing the safety and tolerability in individuals with primary mitochondrial disorders are yet to be published (<a href="https://clinicaltrials.gov/study/NCT03888716" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">NCT03888716</a>). Further studies are needed to investigate the safety and efficacy of these therapies in mtDNA-LSS.</p></div></div><div id="narp.Genetic_Counseling"><h2 id="_narp_Genetic_Counseling_">Genetic Counseling</h2><p>
<i>Genetic counseling is the process of providing individuals and families with
information on the nature, mode(s) of inheritance, and implications of genetic disorders to help them
make informed medical and personal decisions. The following section deals with genetic
risk assessment and the use of family history and genetic testing to clarify genetic
status for family members; it is not meant to address all personal, cultural, or
ethical issues that may arise or to substitute for consultation with a genetics
professional</i>. &#x02014;ED.</p><div id="narp.Mode_of_Inheritance"><h3>Mode of Inheritance</h3><p>Mitochondrial DNA-associated Leigh syndrome spectrum (mtDNA-LSS) is transmitted by mitochondrial (maternal) inheritance.</p></div><div id="narp.Risk_to_Family_Members"><h3>Risk to Family Members</h3><p>
<b>Parents of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a></b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The father of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> is not at risk of having the mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The mother of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> may have the mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> and may exhibit mild clinical manifestations of mtDNA-LSS.</div><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>In most instances, the mother has a much lower <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level (i.e., proportion of abnormal mtDNA) than the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> and usually remains asymptomatic or develops only mild manifestations such as gait disturbance or retinopathy.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Occasionally the mother has a substantial <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level and develops severe manifestations of mtDNA-LSS in adulthood [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.de_vries.1993.410">de Vries et al 1993</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.wei.2018.2388">Wei et al 2018</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>With the exception of the <i>MT-ATP6</i> pathogenic variants <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.8993T&#x0003e;G</a> and <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.8993T&#x0003e;C</a>, a lower <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level in maternal blood does not exclude a higher heteroplasmy level in tissues such as brain or muscle.</div></li></ul></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Many affected individuals have no known family history of mtDNA-LSS or other mitochondrial disorders. The explanation for apparently <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/simplex/">simplex</a> cases may be absence of a comprehensive and/or reliable family history, a maternal <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level below the threshold level that causes disease, or a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> in the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>. A study of 105 probands with childhood-onset mitochondrial disease, including mtDNA-LSS, suggested that approximately 25% of individuals had a <i>de novo</i> pathogenic variant [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.sallevelt.2017.73">Sallevelt et al 2017</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Molecular genetic testing of the mother for the mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> identified in the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> is recommended. Assessment in at least two different maternal tissues (e.g., blood, urine sediment cells, buccal cells) is suggested [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.mavraki.2023.148">Mavraki et al 2023</a>]. Testing of maternal relatives can be considered to provide reassurance or appropriate surveillance and genetic counselling. (Note: Mitochondrial DNA pathogenic variants may be lost from the leukocyte population with increasing age [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.schon.2021.e066288">Schon et al 2021</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.davis.2022.e730">Davis et al 2022</a>]; therefore, in adults with milder manifestations and for asymptomatic older maternal relatives, the pathogenic variant may only be detected in tissues such as hair follicles, urine sediment cells, or skeletal muscle.)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>If the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> represents a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/simplex/">simplex</a> case (i.e., the only family member known to be affected) and the mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> identified in the proband cannot be identified in maternal tissues, possibilities to consider include a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> pathogenic variant in the proband and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> (gonadal) <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/mosaicism/">mosaicism</a> in the mother. Care should be used when determining if a pathogenic variant is <i>de novo</i>. For some pathogenic variants, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homoplasmic/">homoplasmic</a> (or apparently homoplasmic levels) in an affected child may result from very low <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> levels in the mother.</div></li></ul><p><b>Sibs of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>.</b> The risk to the sibs depends on the genetic status of the mother.</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>If the mother of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> has the mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> identified in the proband, all sibs are at risk of inheriting the pathogenic variant. Sibs may inherit the pathogenic variant at varying <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> levels due to the bottleneck effect and variant-specific <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/segregation/">segregation</a> patterns.</div><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>For the <i>MT-ATP6</i> pathogenic variants m.8993T&#x0003e;G and m.8993T&#x0003e;C, a strong positive relationship exists between the mother's <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level and the predicted <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/recurrence-risk/">recurrence risk</a> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.white.1999a.474">White et al 1999a</a>] (see <a class="figpopup" href="/sites/books/NBK1173/figure/narp.F3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-figpopup="fignarpF3" rid-ob="figobnarpF3">Figure 3</a>). However, the 95% confidence interval of the risk estimates are wide, and these data may be of limited use for <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genetic-counseling/">genetic counseling</a>.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Due to positive selection, the m.8993T&#x0003e;G <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> exhibits skewed <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/segregation/">segregation</a> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.otten.2018.1331">Otten et al 2018</a>], with the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> levels in oocytes/embryos generally displaying a distribution to the extremes, either 0% or approximately 100%.</div></li></ul></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The risk to a sib of developing clinical manifestations is difficult to determine and depends on <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level, the variation in heteroplasmy levels among different tissues, and the disease threshold (i.e., the minimum level of heteroplasmy expected to result in mitochondrial disease) for the specific variant (see <a href="#narp.GenotypePhenotype_Correlations">Genotype-Phenotype Correlations</a>).</div><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>In mtDNA-LSS, the <i>MT-ATP6</i> pathogenic variants m.8993T&#x0003e;G and m.8993T&#x0003e;C probably exhibit the strongest <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genotype/">genotype</a>-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> correlation of any mtDNA pathogenic variants [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.ng.2019.310">Ng et al 2019</a>]; they show very little tissue-dependent or age-dependent variation in <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level as well as a strong correlation between heteroplasmy level and clinical outcome.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>It is difficult to use <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level to predict clinical outcome (e.g., in asymptomatic family members or in <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/prenatal-diagnosis/">prenatal diagnosis</a>) for other mtDNA pathogenic variants detected in individuals known to be associated with mtDNA-LSS unless the value is near 0% or near 100%. Retrospective studies for some of the more common mtDNA pathogenic variants can be used to indicate an approximate (empiric) <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/recurrence-risk/">recurrence risk</a>. See <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.chinnery.1998.1889">Chinnery et al [1998]</a> and <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.pickett.2019.1971">Pickett et al [2019]</a> for <i>MT-TL1</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.3243A&#x0003e;G</a> and <i>MT-TK</i> pathogenic variant <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.8344A&#x0003e;G</a> and <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.sallevelt.2017.73">Sallevelt et al [2017]</a>, and <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.steffann.2021.720">Steffann et al [2021]</a>, and <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.vachin.2018.131">Vachin et al [2018]</a> for other mtDNA pathogenic variants.</div></li></ul></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Variation in clinical manifestations is seen among family members with the same <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genotype/">genotype</a> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.stendel.2020.e393">Stendel et al 2020</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>If the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> is presumed to have a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> (i.e., the pathogenic variant is absent from the blood and at least one other sample from the mother and any maternal relatives who were tested), sibs are at low risk [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.sallevelt.2017.73">Sallevelt et al 2017</a>]. However, the possibility of maternal <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/germline/">germline</a> (gonadal) <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/mosaicism/">mosaicism</a> cannot be excluded and therefore the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/recurrence-risk/">recurrence risk</a> is not 0%.</div></li></ul><div class="iconblock whole_rhythm clearfix ten_col fig" id="fignarpF3" co-legend-rid="figlgndnarpF3"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/figure/narp.F3/?report=objectonly" target="object" title="Figure 3. " class="img_link icnblk_img figpopup" rid-figpopup="fignarpF3" rid-ob="figobnarpF3"><img class="small-thumb" src="/sites/books/NBK1173/bin/narp-Image003.gif" src-large="/sites/books/NBK1173/bin/narp-Image003.jpg" alt="Figure 3. . Predicted risk to offspring (95% CI) to develop clinical manifestations of Leigh syndrome spectrum due to MT-ATP6 pathogenic variants m." /></a><div class="icnblk_cntnt" id="figlgndnarpF3"><h4 id="narp.F3"><a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/figure/narp.F3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpF3">Figure 3. </a></h4><p class="float-caption no_bottom_margin">Predicted risk to offspring (95% CI) to develop clinical manifestations of Leigh syndrome spectrum due to <i>MT-ATP6</i> pathogenic variants m.8993T&#x0003e;G or m.8993T&#x0003e;C based on the heteroplasmy level detected in blood in the mother [White et al 1999a] <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/figure/narp.F3/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpF3">(more...)</a></p></div></div><p>
<b>Offspring of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a></b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Offspring of a male <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> with an mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> are not at risk.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>All offspring of a female <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> with an mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> are at risk of inheriting the pathogenic variant. Offspring may inherit the pathogenic variant at varying <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> levels due to the bottleneck effect and variant-specific <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/segregation/">segregation</a> patterns.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The risk to offspring of a female <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> of developing clinical manifestations is difficult to determine and depends on the maternal <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level, the variation in heteroplasmy levels among different tissues in the proband, and the specific mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>. Retrospective studies for some of the most common mtDNA pathogenic variants can be used to indicate an approximate (empiric) <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/recurrence-risk/">recurrence risk</a> for the offspring of females. See <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.white.1999a.474">White et al [1999a]</a> for the <i>MT-ATP6</i> pathogenic variants m.8993T&#x0003e;G and m.8993T&#x0003e;C, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.chinnery.1998.1889">Chinnery et al [1998]</a> and <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.pickett.2019.1971">Pickett et al [2019]</a> for the <i>MT-TL1</i> pathogenic variant m.3243A&#x0003e;G and <i>MT-TK</i> pathogenic variant m.8344A&#x0003e;G, and <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.sallevelt.2017.73">Sallevelt et al [2017]</a> and <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.steffann.2021.720">Steffann et al [2021]</a> for other mtDNA pathogenic variants.</div></li></ul><p><b>Other family members.</b> The risk to other family members depends on the genetic status of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>'s mother: if the proband's mother has an mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>, her sibs and mother are also at risk.</p></div><div id="narp.Related_Genetic_Counseling_Issues"><h3>Related Genetic Counseling Issues</h3><p>
<b>Family planning</b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The optimal time for determination of genetic risk and discussion of the availability of prenatal/<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a> is before pregnancy. Similarly, decisions regarding testing to determine the genetic status of at-risk asymptomatic family members are best made before pregnancy.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>It is appropriate to offer <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genetic-counseling/">genetic counseling</a> (including general discussion of potential risks to offspring and reproductive options) to young adults who are affected or at risk. It should be emphasized in genetic counseling for mtDNA-LSS that it is not possible to make specific predictions about clinical outcome in individuals or their offspring.</div></li></ul><p><b>Phenotypic variability.</b> The <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> of an individual with an mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> results from a combination of factors, including the severity of the pathogenic variant, the percentage of abnormal mitochondria (mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a>), and the organs and tissues in which the pathogenic variant is found (tissue distribution). Family members often inherit different percentages of abnormal mtDNA and therefore can have a wide range of clinical manifestations.</p><p><b>Testing in asymptomatic at-risk family members.</b> Testing to evaluate the genetic status of an apparently asymptomatic family member is technically possible once the mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> has been identified in the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>. However, interpretation of test results of asymptomatic at-risk family members is extremely difficult. Prediction of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> based on test results is not possible. Furthermore, absence of the mtDNA pathogenic variant in one tissue (e.g., blood) does not guarantee that the pathogenic variant is absent in other tissues.</p></div><div id="narp.Prenatal_Testing_and_Preimplantatio"><h3>Prenatal Testing and Preimplantation Genetic Testing</h3><p>Recurrence risk assessment and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/prenatal-testing/">prenatal testing</a> for disorders caused by pathogenic variants in mtDNA are challenging due to the intricacies of mtDNA transmission, such as the mtDNA bottleneck effect (resulting in variation in <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> levels between generations), <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>-specific selection, the threshold effect (the heteroplasmy level associated with clinical manifestations of mitochondrial disease), and the inherent challenge in using prenatal genetic test results to predict clinical outcome.</p><p>The European Neuromuscular Disease Centre International Consensus Workshops have been instrumental in developing guidelines for the counseling of reproductive options for families with mitochondrial disease [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.poulton.2000.460">Poulton &#x00026; Turnbull 2000</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.poulton.2010.559">Poulton &#x00026; Bredenoord 2010</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.poulton.2019.725">Poulton et al 2019</a>].</p><p>Reproductive options for the family members of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> with an mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> may include <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/prenatal-testing/">prenatal testing</a>, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a> (PGT), and oocyte donation. Prenatal testing appears to be the preferred reproductive testing option for mtDNA pathogenic variants that appear to have occurred <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> in the proband and are associated with low <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/recurrence-risk/">recurrence risk</a> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.sallevelt.2017.73">Sallevelt et al 2017</a>], as there is a high likelihood that the pathogenic variant will not be present in prenatal samples. Prenatal testing may also be suitable for women thought to have low recurrence risk due to having non-zero but low levels of the pathogenic mtDNA variant, such as &#x0003c;20% <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a>. PGT is currently considered an appropriate reproductive option for females with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/familial/">familial</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmic/">heteroplasmic</a> mtDNA pathogenic variants and is likely the best option available, at present, for those with moderate recurrence risk. However, PGT may not be successful for women at moderate risk and is not recommended for females containing <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homoplasmic/">homoplasmic</a> or near homoplasmic levels of an mtDNA pathogenic variant [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.poulton.2019.725">Poulton et al 2019</a>]. For families in which prenatal testing and/or PGT are unlikely to successfully reduce recurrence risk, oocyte donation may be considered.</p><p><b>Prenatal testing</b> involves quantification of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level in fetal tissue obtained by chorionic villus biopsy (CVS) (usually performed between 11-12 weeks' gestation) or amniocentesis (usually performed at 15-18 weeks' gestation).</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Available evidence suggests that the proportion of abnormal mtDNA in extraembryonic and embryonic tissues is similar and does not change substantially during pregnancy. For a number of mtDNA pathogenic variants, including m.8993T&#x0003e;G, m.8993T&#x0003e;C, and m.9176T&#x0003e;C in <i>MT-ATP6</i>, m.3243A&#x0003e;G in <i>MT-TL1</i>, m.10191T&#x0003e;C in <i>MT-ND3</i>, and m.13513G&#x0003e;A in <i>MT-ND5</i>, the proportion of abnormal mtDNA has been reported to remain relatively stable throughout embryofetal development and in postnatal tissue [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.white.1999a.474">White et al 1999a</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.dahl.2000.246">Dahl et al 2000</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.thorburn.2001.102">Thorburn &#x00026; Dahl 2001</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.jacobs.2005.223">Jacobs et al 2005</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.steffann.2007.664">Steffann et al 2007</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.monnot.2011.116">Monnot et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.steffann.2021.720">Steffann et al 2021</a>]. However, variation in mtDNA <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> levels between the placenta and embryonic/postnatal tissue or between multiple placental samples has also been reported [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.marchington.2006.816">Marchington et al 2006</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.monnot.2011.116">Monnot et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.vachin.2018.131">Vachin et al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.steffann.2021.720">Steffann et al 2021</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>There is no consensus between centers regarding the preference for CVS or amniocentesis for <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/prenatal-testing/">prenatal testing</a> for mtDNA disease, although some studies support the use of amniocentesis given reports of intraplacental variation with CVS [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.steffann.2021.720">Steffann et al 2021</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Analysis should be done on direct CVS/amniocytes, not on cultured cells, as the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level of mtDNA pathogenic variants can change during cell culture.</div></li></ul><p>The major limitation of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/prenatal-testing/">prenatal testing</a> is the potential difficulty in predicting clinical outcome based on fetal genetic testing results. For prediction of clinical outcome to be reliable, there must be a close correlation between the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> level and disease severity; uniform distribution of heteroplasmy levels in all tissues; and stability of the heteroplasmy level over time [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.poulton.2000.460">Poulton &#x00026; Turnbull 2000</a>]. See <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">Genotype-Phenotype Correlations</a> for <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>-specific information.</p><p>Clinical practice varies in determining which thresholds are used to determine the likelihood a fetus will be affected or unaffected based on prenatal test results. A systematic review suggested a generic threshold of 18% after it was found that individuals with muscle <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> levels below approximately 18% had a 95% chance or higher of being unaffected, independent of the mtDNA variant [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.hellebrekers.2012.341">Hellebrekers et al 2012</a>]. Prenatal heteroplasmy risk groups of low (&#x0003c;30%), intermediate (30%-60%) and high (&#x0003e;60%) have been applied in different studies [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.nesbitt.2014.1255">Nesbitt et al 2014</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.vachin.2018.131">Vachin et al 2018</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.steffann.2021.720">Steffann et al 2021</a>]. An intermediate proportion of abnormal DNA would represent a "gray zone" in which interpretation is difficult or impossible. For some pathogenic variants, using this risk stratification is feasible; however, it may not be appropriate for other pathogenic variants, such as m.3243A&#x0003e;G in <i>MT-TL1</i> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.hellebrekers.2012.341">Hellebrekers et al 2012</a>].</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>While the <i>MT-ATP6</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> m.8993T&#x0003e;G is known to exhibit skewed <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/segregation/">segregation</a> in embryos or oocytes (either 0% or approximately 100%) [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.blok.1997.1495">Blok et al 1997</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.otten.2018.1331">Otten et al 2018</a>], <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/prenatal-testing/">prenatal testing</a> is still considered a suitable option for females with low <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> levels given that a reliable prediction is possible for most heteroplasmy levels [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.smeets.2015.29">Smeets et al 2015</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>A model has been developed for the <i>MT-TL1</i> m.3243A&#x0003e;G <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> to facilitate predictions of fetal <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> (below a specified threshold of 18%) based on maternal heteroplasmy levels [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.pickett.2019.1971">Pickett et al 2019</a>].</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>For other mtDNA pathogenic variants, <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/prenatal-diagnosis/">prenatal diagnosis</a> may be offered to females with a low proportion of abnormal mtDNA. However, weaker <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genotype/">genotype</a>-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> correlation or lack of data would mean that accurate prediction of the phenotype may not possible. Couples would require careful counseling before embarking on these procedures.</div></li></ul><p><b>Preimplantation genetic testing (PGT)</b> involves quantification of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmic/">heteroplasmic</a> variant load to facilitate selection of the most appropriate embryo for transfer. PGT is traditionally performed by sampling one or two blastomeres on day three or four (cleavage stage) after fertilization. At the cleavage stage, there are robust data supporting the correlation between the heteroplasmic variant load in individual blastomeres and the whole embryo [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.steffann.2006.244">Steffann et al 2006</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.monnot.2011.116">Monnot et al 2011</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.sallevelt.2013.125">Sallevelt et al 2013</a>]. Most centers offering PGT for inherited diseases now use trophectoderm biopsy rather than cleavage stage biopsy. There are limited data using trophectoderm biopsy for mtDNA PGT, with one study suggesting it may be less suitable for estimating <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> in the embryo [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.mitalipov.2014.935">Mitalipov et al 2014</a>], although this remains unclear [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.poulton.2019.725">Poulton et al 2019</a>].</p><p>Embryos should only be regarded as suitable for implantation if they have a very low proportion of abnormal mtDNA, preferably 0%, although threshold levels of 18% may imply low <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/recurrence-risk/">recurrence risk</a> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.hellebrekers.2012.341">Hellebrekers et al 2012</a>, <a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.smeets.2015.29">Smeets et al 2015</a>]. Nevertheless, as disease thresholds and patterns of transmission vary with different mtDNA pathogenic variants, it is important to assess this on an individual basis, exploring the couple's own views on what level of residual risk they are willing to accept. Heteroplasmy levels of clinically affected and unaffected individuals within a family should also be considered [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.poulton.2019.725">Poulton et al 2019</a>].</p><p>In some females, particularly those with a high proportion of abnormal mtDNA in blood or urine sediment cells, a large proportion of oocytes may have a high level of abnormal mtDNA, and multiple cycles of ovarian stimulation may not result in an embryo suitable for implantation. However, PGT for mtDNA pathogenic variants may provide valuable information for future reproductive planning even if a successful unaffected conception is not achieved.</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>If most of the embryos tested have a substantial proportion of abnormal mtDNA, oocyte donation is likely to be the only current option for ensuring an unaffected embryo.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>In contrast, if most of the embryos tested have undetectable abnormal mtDNA, the parents may opt for <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/prenatal-testing/">prenatal testing</a> in subsequent unassisted (natural) pregnancies.</div></li></ul><div id="narp.Other_Reproductive_Options"><h4>Other Reproductive Options</h4><p><b>Oocyte donation accompanied by IVF using the partner's sperm.</b> Use of a maternal relative as the oocyte donor should be avoided, since the relative may have oocytes with a high proportion of abnormal mtDNA even though her leukocytes may lack detectable abnormal mtDNA.</p><p><b>Mitochondrial replacement therapy (MRT)</b> involves the transfer of a nucleus removed from either a zygote (pronuclear transfer) or an oocyte (maternal spindle transfer) into an enucleated cell at the same embryonic stage from an unaffected donor. Clinical trials are currently under way to investigate whether this reproductive option would be suitable for females with pathogenic mtDNA variants where PGT is not likely to be (or has not been) an effective option (e.g., <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/homoplasmic/">homoplasmic</a> variants and/or <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmic/">heteroplasmic</a> variants at high <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heteroplasmy/">heteroplasmy</a> levels).</p><p>The first child born after maternal spindle transfer was reported in 2017, born to a female with <i>MT-ATP6</i> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> m.8993T&#x0003e;G [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.zhang.2017.361">Zhang et al 2017</a>]. The boy was reported to be well at age seven months but long-term neurodevelopmental follow up has yet to be published.</p><p>After extensive scientific, ethical, and public consultation, in 2016, the UK government gave permission for MRT to prevent the transmission of severe mitochondrial disease caused by specific mtDNA pathogenic variants [<a class="bk_pop" href="#narp.REF.herbert.2017.171">Herbert &#x00026; Turnbull 2017</a>]. The use of MRT in the UK is regulated by the Human Fertilisation and Embryology Authority (HFEA) and is limited to families who are at substantial risk for transmitting severe mtDNA disease in a situation in which PGT is deemed inappropriate or likely to be unsuccessful. A five-year clinical trial is being carried out at the Newcastle Fertility Clinic, and outcomes are yet to be published. In 2022, the Australian government passed the Mitochondrial Donation Law Reform Bill (Maeve's Law) regarding the clinical implementation of MRT. Under the bill, MRT may be introduced in a staged manner, with current legislation allowing for the commencement of a clinical trial of the use of MRT techniques (see <a href="https://www.nhmrc.gov.au/mitochondrial-donation" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">National Health and Medical Research Council</a>).</p></div></div></div><div id="narp.Resources"><h2 id="_narp_Resources_">Resources</h2><p>

<a href="/omim/590060" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">590060</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">TRANSFER RNA, MITOCHONDRIAL, LYSINE; MTTK</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/590095" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">590095</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">TRANSFER RNA, MITOCHONDRIAL, TRYPTOPHAN; MTTW</td></tr><tr><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">
<a href="/omim/590105" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=entrez&amp;targetcat=term&amp;targettype=omim">590105</a></td><td rowspan="1" colspan="1" style="text-align:left;vertical-align:top;">TRANSFER RNA, MITOCHONDRIAL, VALINE; MTTV</td></tr></tbody></table></div></div><div id="narp.Molecular_Pathogenesis"><h3>Molecular Pathogenesis</h3><p>Human mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) encodes 37 genes, including 13 genes encoding protein subunits of the mitochondrial respiratory chain and oxidative phosphorylation system, 22 transfer RNA (tRNA) genes, and two ribosomal RNA (rRNA) genes. All mtDNA genes lack introns and are transcribed as large polycistronic transcripts that are processed into monocistronic mRNAs. Protein-coding genes are then translated by the mitochondrial-specific translational machinery. The mitochondrial-specific translational machinery is required because translation of mtDNA-encoded genes is physically separated from the cytosolic translational machinery and because the mtDNA genetic code differs from the universal genetic code in several codons.</p><p>For some mtDNA pathogenic variants associated with Leigh syndrome spectrum (LSS), a strong correlation exists between the proportion of abnormal-to-<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/wild_type/">wild type</a> mtDNA and severity of the biochemical <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/phenotype/">phenotype</a> in cultured cells. For some variants, such as <i>MT-ATP6</i> pathogenic variants <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.8993T&#x0003e;G</a> and <a href="/sites/books/NBK1173/table/narp.T.pathogenic_variants_referenced_in/?report=objectonly" target="object" rid-ob="figobnarpTpathogenicvariantsreferencedin">m.8993T&#x0003e;C</a>, a strong correlation also exists between the proportion of abnormal-to-wild type mtDNA and clinical severity. However, the mechanism by which some mtDNA pathogenic variants cause a phenotype such as LSS, while others cause myopathy, deafness, or diabetes mellitus, is unknown.</p><p>Pathogenic mtDNA variants causing LSS fall into two major classes, namely, those in tRNA genes and those in protein-coding genes.</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Transfer RNA pathogenic variants cause decreased mitochondrial protein synthesis by abnormalities in base modification and aminoacylation of the tRNA.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Pathogenic variants in protein-coding mtDNA genes typically cause decreased activity of the respiratory chain complex of which that subunit is a part.</div></li></ul><p><b>Mechanism of disease causation.</b> Loss of function</p><div See <a href="/sites/books/n/gene/app3/">Quick Reference</a> for an explanation of nomenclature.</p></div></dd><dt></dt><dd><div><p class="no_margin">See <a href="https://mitomap.org/MITOMAP" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">MITOMAP</a> for details of mitochondrial variants.</p></div></dd></dl></div></div></div></div></div><div id="narp.Chapter_Notes"><h2 id="_narp_Chapter_Notes_">Chapter Notes</h2><div id="narp.Author_Notes"><h3>Author Notes</h3><p>Prof Rahman, Prof Thorburn, and Dr Ball are actively involved in clinical research regarding individuals with mitochondrial DNA-associated Leigh syndrome spectrum (mtDNA-LSS). Prof Thorburn is a member of the <a href="https://www.monash.edu/medicine/mitohope/home" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">mitoHOPE Program</a> team performing an Australian clinical trial of mitochondrial replacement therapy.</p><p>The authors would be happy to communicate with persons who have any questions regarding diagnosis of mtDNA-LSS or other considerations.</p></div><div id="narp.Acknowledgments"><h3>Acknowledgments</h3><p>The authors would like to acknowledge the support of clinicians, researchers, and patient advocacy groups for mtDNA-LSS. They would also like to acknowledge all the patients and families who have generously given their time and shared their experiences to advance the understanding of mtDNA-LSS.</p></div><div id="narp.Author_History"><h3>Author History</h3><p>Megan Ball, MD (2024-present)<br />Joyeeta Rahman, BSc; UCL Great Ormond Street Institute of Child Health (2017-2024)<br />Shamima Rahman, PhD, FRCP, FRCPCH (2003-present)<br />David R Thorburn, PhD, FHGSA, FFSc(RCPA) (2003-present)</p></div><div id="narp.Revision_History"><h3>Revision History</h3><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>9 May 2024 (sw) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>28 September 2017 (sw) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>17 April 2014 (me) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>8 February 2011 (me) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>3 February 2006 (me) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>30 October 2003 (me) Review posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>3 July 2003 (dt) Original submission</div></li></ul></div></div><div id="narp.References"><h2 id="_narp_References_">References</h2><div id="narp.Published_Guidelines__Consensus_Sta"><h3>Published Guidelines / Consensus Statements</h3><ul class="simple-list"><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Diodato D, Schiff M, Cohen BH, Bertini E, Rahman S; Workshop participants. 258th ENMC international workshop Leigh syndrome spectrum: genetic causes, natural history and preparing for clinical trials 25-27 March 2022, Hoofddorp, Amsterdam, The Netherlands.
